\section*{681. On the Derivatives of Three Binary Quantics.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{681. On the Derivatives of Three Binary Quantics}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 157---168.]

For a reason which will appear, instead of the ordinary factorial notation, I write $\{a012\}$ to denote the factorial $a \cdot (a+1) \cdot (a+2)$, and so in other cases; and I consider the series of equations
\begin{align*}
(1) &= X, \\
(2) &= (\{a0\}, \{\beta0\} \,\,\,\, Y, -Y'), \\
(3) &= (\{a01\}, 2\{a1\}\{\beta1\}, \{\beta01\} \,\,\,\, Z, -Z', Z''), \\
(4) &= (\{a012\}, 3\{a12\}\{\beta2\}, 3\{a2\}\{\beta12\}, \{\beta012\} \,\,\,\, W, -W', W'', -W'''), \text{ \&c.}
\end{align*}
where
\[
X = Y + Y', \quad Y = Z + Z', \quad Y' = Z' + Z'', \quad Z = W + W', \quad Z' = W' + W'', \quad Z'' = W'' + W''', \quad \text{\&c.}
\]
We have thus a series of linear equations serving to determine $X$; $Y$, $Y'$; $Z$, $Z'$, $Z''$; $W$, $W'$, $W''$, $W'''$; \&c.

We require in particular the values of $X$; $Y$, $Y'$; $Z$, $Z''$; $W$, $W'''$; \&c., and I write down the results as follow:
\begin{align*}
X &= (1), \\
\{a + \beta\}Y &= \{a0\} \cdot (+1), \\
\{ \quad \}Y' &= \{a0\} \cdot (-1); \\
\{a + \beta 2\}(1), \quad \{a + \beta 1\}(2), \quad &\{a + \beta 0\}(3) \\
\{a + \beta 012\}Z &= \{a01\}, \quad +2\{\beta 1\}, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}Z' &= \{a0\}\{\beta0\}, \quad a - \beta, \quad -1, \\
\{ \quad \}Z'' &= \{a01\}, \quad -2\{a1\}, \quad +1;
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 34\}(1), \quad \{a + \beta 14\}(2), \quad &\{a + \beta 03\}(3), \quad \{a + \beta 01\}(4) \\
\{a + \beta 01 \ldots 4\}W &= \{\beta 012\}, \quad +3\{\beta 12\}, \quad +3\{\beta 2\}, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W' &= \{a0\}\{\beta 01\}, \quad 2a - \beta, \quad a - 2\beta - 2, \quad -1, \\
\{ \quad \}W'' &= \{a01\}\{\beta 0\}, \quad a - 2\beta, \quad -2a + \beta - 2, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W''' &= \{a012\}, \quad -3\{a12\}, \quad +3\{a2\}, \quad -1;
\end{align*}
\&c.

\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta\}X &= \beta(1) + (2), \\
\{a + \beta\}X' &= a(1) - (2), \\
\{a + \beta\}\{a + \beta + 1\}\{a + \beta + 2\}Z &= \beta \cdot \{\beta + 1\} \cdot \{a + \beta + 2\} \cdot (1) + 2\{\beta + 1\} \cdot \{a + \beta + 1\} \cdot (2) + \{a + \beta\} \cdot (3), \\
\{ \quad \}Z'' &= a \cdot \{a + 1\} \cdot \{a + \beta + 2\} \cdot (1) + 2\{a + 1\} \cdot \{a + \beta + 1\} \cdot (2) + \{a + \beta\} \cdot (3),
\end{align*}
\&c., the law being obvious, except as regards the numbers which in the top lines occur in connexion with $a + \beta$ in the $\{\}$ symbols. As regards these, we form them by successive subtractions as shown by the diagrams
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccccccc}
 & 34 & & 456 & & 5678 \\
 & 2 & & 3 & & 4 \\
14 & & 156 & & 1678 \\
1 & & 03 & & 12 & & 13 \\
 & 036 & & 0378 \\
2 & & 01 & & 21 & & 22 \\
 & 015 & & 0158 \\
3 & & 012 & & 31 & & 4 \\
 & 0127 & & 0123
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
and the statement of the result is now complete.

In part verification, starting from the $Y$-formulas (which are obtained at once),
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 2\}(1), \quad \{a + \beta 1\}(2), \quad &\{a + \beta 0\}(3), \quad \{a + \beta 012\} \\
\{ \quad \}Z &= X - \mu - \nu, \\
\{ \quad \}Z' &= X' - \mu' - \nu',
\end{align*}
assume we must have
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 2\} \cdot X + X' &= \{a + \beta 012\} \cdot Y, \\
\{ \quad \} \cdot X' + X'' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot Y', \text{ \&c.},
\end{align*}
that is,
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 2\} \cdot X + X' &= \{a + \beta 12\} (\{\beta 0\} + Y), \\
\{ \quad \} \cdot X' + X'' &= \{ \quad \} (\{a0\} \cdot -1), \text{ \&c.}
\end{align*}

And further
\[
\{a + \beta 2\}(\{a01\}, -2\{a1\}\{\beta1\}, \{\beta01\} \,\,\,\, X, X', X'') = 0,
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\begin{align*}
X + X' &= \{a + \beta 1\} \cdot \{\beta 0\}, \\
X' + X'' &= \{ \quad \} \{a0\}.
\end{align*}

And in like manner we have
\begin{align*}
\mu + \mu' &= \{a + \beta 2\} \cdot 1, \\
\mu' + \mu'' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot -1,
\end{align*}
\[
(\{a01\}, -2\{a1\}\{\beta1\}, \{\beta01\} \,\,\,\, \mu, \mu', \mu'') = 0;
\]
and
\begin{align*}
\nu + \nu' &= 0, \\
(\{a01\}, -2\{a1\}\{\beta1\}, \{\beta01\} \,\,\,\, \nu, \nu', \nu'') &= 0.
\end{align*}

We hence find without difficulty
\begin{align*}
\lambda, \mu, \nu &= \beta \cdot \beta + 1, \quad 2\beta + 1, \quad +1, \\
\lambda', \mu', \nu' &= a \cdot \beta, \quad a - \beta, \quad -1, \\
\lambda'', \mu'', \nu'' &= a \cdot a + 1, \quad -2 \cdot a + 1, \quad +1;
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
&= \{\beta 01\}, \quad 2\{\beta 1\}, \quad +1, \\
&= \{a0\}\{\beta 0\}, \quad a - \beta, \quad -1, \\
&= \{a01\}, \quad -2\{a1\}, \quad +1;
\end{align*}
viz. for verification of the $X$-equations we have
\begin{align*}
\beta \cdot \{\beta + 1\} \cdot + a \cdot \beta, \text{ that is, } \{a + \beta + 1\} \cdot \beta, &= \{a + \beta 1\} \cdot \{\beta 0\}, \\
a \cdot \beta \cdot + a \cdot \{a + 1\}, \text{ `` } \{a + 1 + \beta\} \cdot a, &= \{ \quad \} \{a0\}, \\
(a \cdot \{a + 1\}, -2 \cdot \{a + 1\} \cdot \{\beta + 1\}, \quad &\beta \cdot \{\beta + 1\} \\
a \cdot \beta, \quad a \cdot \{a + 1\}) &= 0, \\
a \cdot \{a + 1\} \cdot \beta \cdot \{\beta + 1\} - 2 \cdot \{a + 1\} \cdot \{\beta + 1\} \cdot a \cdot \beta + \beta \cdot \{\beta + 1\} \cdot a \cdot \{a + 1\} &= 0;
\end{align*}
and similarly the $\mu$- and $\nu$-equations may be verified.

We have thus for the $Z$'s the equations
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 2\}(1), \quad \{a + \beta 1\}(2), \quad &\{a + \beta 0\}(3) \\
\{a + \beta 012\}Z &= \{a01\}, \quad +2\{\beta 1\}, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}Z' &= \{a0\}\{\beta 0\}, \quad a - \beta, \quad -1, \\
\{ \quad \}Z'' &= \{a01\}, \quad -2\{a1\}, \quad +1,
\end{align*}
which include the foregoing expressions for $Z$ and $Z''$.

We may then take the expressions for the $W$'s to be
\begin{align*}
\{a + \beta 34\}(1), \quad \{a + \beta 14\}(2), \quad \{a + \beta 03\}(3), \quad &\{a + \beta 01\}(4) \\
\{a + \beta 0123\}W &= \{\beta 012\}, \quad +3\{\beta 12\}, \quad +3\{\beta 2\}, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W' &= \lambda', \quad \mu', \quad \nu', \quad -1, \\
\{ \quad \}W'' &= \lambda'', \quad \mu'', \quad \nu'', \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W''' &= \{a012\}, \quad -3\{a12\}, \quad +3\{a2\}, \quad -1;
\end{align*}
and that is,
\begin{align*}
\{ \quad \}W &= \lambda, \quad \mu, \quad \nu, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W' &= \lambda', \quad \mu', \quad \nu', \quad -1, \\
\{ \quad \}W'' &= \lambda'', \quad \mu'', \quad \nu'', \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}W''' &= \lambda''', \quad \mu''', \quad \nu''', \quad -1;
\end{align*}
and we obtain in like manner the equations
\begin{align*}
X + X' &= \{a + \beta 234\} \cdot \{\beta 01\}, \\
X' + X'' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot \{a0\} \cdot \{\beta 0\}, \\
X'' + X''' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot \{a01\},
\end{align*}
\[
(\{a012\}, -3\{a12\}\{\beta2\}, +3\{a2\}\{\beta12\}, -\{\beta012\} \,\,\,\, X, X', X'', X''') = 0;
\]
\begin{align*}
\mu + \mu' &= \{a + \beta 134\} \cdot 2\{\beta 1\}, \\
\mu' + \mu'' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot a - \beta, \\
\mu'' + \mu''' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot -2\{a1\},
\end{align*}
\[
(\{a012\}, -3\{a12\}\{\beta2\}, +3\{a2\}\{\beta12\}, -\{\beta012\} \,\,\,\, \mu, \mu', \mu'', \mu''') = 0;
\]
\begin{align*}
\nu + \nu' &= \{a + \beta 034\} \cdot 1, \\
\nu' + \nu'' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot -1, \\
\nu'' + \nu''' &= \{ \quad \} \cdot 1,
\end{align*}
\[
(\{a012\}, -3\{a12\}\{\beta2\}, +3\{a2\}\{\beta12\}, -\{\beta012\} \,\,\,\, \nu, \nu', \nu'', \nu''') = 0;
\]
\begin{align*}
\rho + \rho' &= 0, \\
\rho' + \rho'' &= 0, \\
\rho'' + \rho''' &= 0,
\end{align*}
\[
(\{a012\}, -3\{a12\}\{\beta2\}, +3\{a2\}\{\beta12\}, -\{\beta012\} \,\,\,\, \rho, \rho', \rho'', \rho''') = \{a + \beta 01234\}.
\]
These give for the $\lambda'''$, square the values
\begin{align*}
&\{\beta 012\}, \quad 3\{\beta 12\}, \quad 3\{\beta 2\}, \quad +1, \\
&\{a0\}\{\beta 01\}, \quad 2a - \beta \cdot \{\beta 1\}, \quad a - 2\beta - 2, \quad -1, \\
&\{a01\}\{\beta 0\}, \quad a - 2\beta \cdot \{a1\}, \quad -2a + \beta - 2, \quad +1, \\
&\{a012\}, \quad -3\{a12\}, \quad +3\{a2\}, \quad -1,
\end{align*}
and so on; the law however of the terms in the intermediate lines is not by any means obvious.

Consider now the binary quantics $P$, $Q$, $R$, of the forms $(\xi x, y)^p$, $(\xi x, y)^q$, $(\xi x, y)^r$; we have for any, for instance for the fourth, order, the derivates
\[
P(Q, R)^4, \quad (P, (Q, R)^2)^2, \quad (P, (Q, R)^2)^2, \quad (P, (Q, R)^4);
\]
and it is required to express $Q(P, R)^4$ and $R(P, Q)^4$, each of them as a linear function of these.

I recall that we have $(P, Q)^2 = PQ$, so that the first and the last terms of the series might have been written $(P, (Q, R)^4)^4$ and $(P, (Q, R)^4)^4$ respectively; and, further, that $(P, Q)^2$ denotes $d_x^2 P \cdot d_y^2 Q - 2d_x d_y P \cdot d_x d_y Q + d_y^2 P \cdot d_x^2 Q$; $(P, Q)^4$ denotes $d_x^4 P \cdot d_y^4 Q - 4d_x^3 d_y P \cdot d_x d_y^3 Q + 6d_x^2 d_y^2 P \cdot d_x^2 d_y^2 Q - 4d_x d_y^3 P \cdot d_x^3 d_y Q + d_y^4 P \cdot d_x^4 Q$, and so on.

I write $(a, b, c, d, e)$ for the fourth derived functions of any quantic $U = (\xi x, y)^m$; we have, in a notation which will be at once understood,
\begin{align*}
U &= (a, b, c, d, e \,\,\,\, x, y)^4 \div [m]^4, \\
(d_x, d_y)U &= (a, b, c, d \,\,\,\, x, y)^3 \div [m-1]^3, \\
(d_x, d_y)^2 U &= (a, b, c \,\,\,\, x, y)^2 \div [m-2]^2, \\
(d_x, d_y)^3 U &= (a, b \,\,\,\, x, y)^1 \div [m-3]^1, \\
(d_x, d_y)^4 U &= (a, b, c, d, e).
\end{align*}
And then, taking
\begin{align*}
&(a_1, b_1, c_1, d_1, e_1), \quad (a_2, b_2, c_2, d_2, e_2), \quad (a_3, b_3, c_3, d_3, e_3),
\end{align*}
to belong to $P$, $Q$, $R$, respectively, we must, instead of $m$, write $p$, $q$, $r$ for the three functions respectively.

If we attend only to the highest terms in $x$, we have
\begin{align*}
U &= ax^m \div [m]^m, \\
(d_x, d_y)U &= (a, b)x^{m-1} \div [m-1]^{m-1}, \\
(d_x, d_y)^2 U &= (a, b, c)x^{m-2} \div [m-2]^{m-2}, \\
(d_x, d_y)^3 U &= (a, b, c, d)x^{m-3} \div [m-3]^{m-3}, \\
(d_x, d_y)^4 U &= (a, b, c, d, e).
\end{align*}

Consider now $P(Q, R)^4$, $(P, (Q, R)^2)^2$, \&c.; in each case attending only to the term in $a_1$, and in this term to the highest term in $x$, we have
\begin{align*}
(1)\quad [p]^4 P(Q, R)^4 &= a_1 e_3 - 4b_1 d_3 + 6c_1 c_3 - 4d_1 b_3 + e_1 a_3 \quad (X), \\
(2)\quad [p-1]^2 [q-3]^2 [r-3]^2 (P, (Q, R)^2)^2 &= [q-3]^2 \cdot (b_1 d_3 - 3c_1 c_3 + 3d_1 b_3 - e_1 a_3) \cdot (-Y') \\
&\quad + [r-3]^2 \cdot (a_1 e_3 - 3b_1 d_3 + 3c_1 c_3 - d_1 b_3) \cdot (Y), \\
(3)\quad [p-2]^2 [q-2]^2 [r-2]^2 (P, (Q, R)^2)^2 &= [q-2]^2 \cdot (c_1 a_3 - 2d_1 b_3 + e_1 a_3) \cdot (Z'') \\
&\quad + 2[q-2]^1[r-2]^1 \cdot (b_1 d_3 - 2c_1 c_3 + d_1 b_3) \cdot (-Z') \\
&\quad + [r-2]^2 \cdot (a_1 e_3 - 2b_1 d_3 + c_1 c_3) \cdot (Z), \\
(4)\quad [p-3]^2 [q-1]^2 [r-1]^2 (P, (Q, R)^1)^3 &= [q-1]^2 \cdot (d_1 b_3 - c_1 a_3) \cdot (-W'') \\
&\quad + 3[q-1]^1[r-1]^1 \cdot (c_1 c_3 - d_1 b_3) \cdot (W''') \\
&\quad + 3[q-1]^1[r-1]^1 \cdot (b_1 d_3 - c_1 c_3) \cdot (-W') \\
&\quad + [r-1]^2 \cdot (a_1 e_3 - b_1 d_3) \cdot (W), \\
(5)\quad [q]^4 [r]^4 R(P, Q)^4 &= [r]^4 \cdot (-U'''') + 4[r]^3[q]^1 \cdot (U''') + 6[r]^2[q]^2 \cdot (-U'') \\
&\quad + 4[r]^1[q]^3 \cdot (U') + [q]^4 \cdot (U).
\end{align*}

Thus, for the second of these equations,
\[
(P, (Q, R)^2)^2 = d_x^2 P \cdot d_y^2 (Q, R)^2 - \text{\&c.}
\]
the term in $a_1$ is $d_y^2 (Q, R)^2 = (d_x Q, R)^2 + (Q, d_y R)^2$, the whole being divided by $[p-1]^2$; where attending only to the highest terms in $x$, the two terms are respectively
\[
(b_2 d_3 - 3c_2 c_3 + 3d_2 b_3 - e_2 a_3) \div [r-3]^2
\]
and
\[
(a_2 e_3 - 3b_2 d_3 + 3c_2 c_3 - d_2 b_3) \div [q-3]^2
\]
which are each divided by $[p-1]^2$ as above; whence, multiplying by $[p-1]^2 [q-1]^2 [r-1]^2$, we have the formula in question; and so for the other cases.

Writing now $(1)$, $(2)$, $(3)$, $(4)$, $(5)$ for the left-hand sides of the five equations respectively; and $-Y'$, $Y$; $Z''$, $-Z'$, $Z$; $-W'''$, $W''$, $-W'$, $W$; $U''''$, $-U'''$, $U''$, $-U'$, $U$; for the literal parts on the right-hand sides of the same equations respectively, then we have $X = Y + Y'$, $Y = Z + Z'$, $Y' = Z' + Z''$, \&c., and the equations become
\begin{align*}
(1) &= X, \\
(2) &= [r-3]^2 Y - [q-3]^2 Y', \\
(3) &= [r-2]^2 Z - 2[r-2]^1[q-2]^1 Z' + [q-2]^2 Z'', \\
(4) &= [r-1]^3 W - 3[r-1]^2[q-1]^1 W' + 3[r-1]^1[q-1]^2 W'' - [q-1]^3 W''', \\
(5) &= [r]^4 U - 4[r]^3[q]^1 U' + 6[r]^2[q]^2 U'' - 4[r]^1[q]^3 U''' + [q]^4 U'''';
\end{align*}
which are, in fact, the equations considered at the beginning of the present paper, putting therein $a = r-3$ and $\beta = q-3$; they consequently give
\begin{align*}
\{q + r - 6, 456\}(1), \quad &\{q + r - 6, 156\}(2), \quad \{q + r - 6, 036\}(3), \\
\{q + r - 6, 015\}(4), \quad &\{q + r - 6, 012\}(5),
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
\{q + r - 6, 01 \ldots 6\}U &= \{q - 3, 0123\}, \quad +4\{q - 3, 123\}, \quad +6\{q - 3, 23\}, \\
&\quad +4\{q - 3, 3\}, \quad +1, \\
\{ \quad \}U'''' &= \{r - 3, 0123\}, \quad -4\{r - 3, 123\}, \quad +6\{r - 3, 23\}, \\
&\quad -4\{r - 3, 3\}, \quad +1.
\end{align*}

Also, attending as before only to the terms in $a_1$, and therein to the highest power of $X$, we have
\[
[q]^4 Q(R, P)^4 = a_1 e_3 \cdot 5 \div [r]^4; \text{ that is, } [q]^4 Q(R, P)^4 = U,
\]
\[
[r]^4 R(P, Q)^4 = U'''';
\]
and, observing that $\{q + r - 6, 01 \ldots 6\}$ is $= [q + r]^5$, and that $\{q + r - 6, 456\}$, \&c., may be written $\{q - r, 210\}$, \&c., where the superscript bars are the signs $-$, the formulae become
\begin{align*}
[q + r]^5 [q]^4 Q(P, R)^4 &= [q]^4 \cdot (+1), \quad +4[q]^3 \cdot (+1), \quad +6[q]^2 \cdot (+1), \\
&\quad +4[q]^1 \cdot (+1), \quad +1, \\
[q + r]^5 [r]^4 R(P, Q)^4 &= [r]^4 \cdot (+1), \quad -4[r]^3 \cdot (+1), \quad +6[r]^2 \cdot (+1), \\
&\quad -4[r]^1 \cdot (+1), \quad +1.
\end{align*}

Written at full length, the first of these equations (which, as being the fourth in a series, I mark 4th equation) is
\begin{multline*}
[q + r]^5 [q]^4 Q(P, R)^4 = \\
1 \cdot \{q + r\} \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot \{q + r - 2\} \cdot [p]^4 [q]^4 \cdot P(Q, R)^4 \quad \text{(4th equat.)} \\
+ 4 \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot \{q + r - 5\} \cdot [p - 1]^2 [q]^2 [q - 2]^1 [r - 2]^1 \cdot (P, (Q, R)^2)^2 \\
+ 6 \cdot \{q + r\} \cdot \{q + r - 3\} \cdot \{q + r - 6\} \cdot [p - 2]^2 [q]^2 [q - 2]^2 [r - 2]^2 \cdot (P, (Q, R)^2)^2 \\
+ 4 \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot \{q + r - 5\} \cdot \{q + r - 6\} \cdot [p - 3]^2 [q]^2 [q - 1]^1 [r - 1]^1 \cdot (P, (Q, R)^1)^3 \\
+ 1 \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot \{q + r - 5\} \cdot \{q + r - 6\} \cdot [q]^4 [r]^4 \cdot R(P, Q)^4,
\end{multline*}
and the other is, in fact, the same equation with $q$, $Q$, $r$, $R$ interchanged with $r$, $R$, $q$, $Q$; the alternate $+$ and $-$ signs arise evidently from the terms $(R, Q)^2 = -(Q, R)^2$; $(R, Q)^4 = (Q, R)^4$; \&c., which present themselves on the right-hand side.

It will be observed that the identity has been derived from the comparison of the terms in $a_1$, which are the highest terms in $x$; the other terms not having been written down or considered; but it is easy to see that an identity of the form in question exists, and, this being admitted, the process is a legitimate one.

The preceding equations of the series are
\begin{align*}
[q + r]^1 [q]^1 Q(P, R)^1 &= 1 \cdot [p]^1 [q]^1 P(Q, R)^1 \quad \text{(1st equation)} \\
&\quad + 1 \cdot [q]^1 [r]^1 (P, Q, R)^1; \\
[q + r]^2 [q]^2 Q(P, R)^2 &= 1 \cdot \{q + r\} \cdot [p]^2 [q]^2 P(Q, R)^2 \quad \text{(2nd equation)} \\
&\quad + 2 \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot [p - 1]^2 [q]^1 [q - 1]^1 [r - 1]^1 (P, (Q, R)^1)^1 \\
&\quad + 1 \cdot \{q + r - 2\} \cdot [q]^2 [r]^2 (P, Q, R)^2; \\
[q + r]^3 [q]^3 Q(P, R)^3 &= 1 \cdot \{q + r\} \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot [p]^3 [q]^3 P(Q, R)^3 \quad \text{(3rd equation)} \\
&\quad + 3 \cdot \{q + r\} \cdot \{q + r - 3\} \cdot [p - 1]^2 [q]^2 [q - 2]^1 [r - 2]^1 (P, (Q, R)^1)^2 \\
&\quad + 2 \cdot \{q + r - 1\} \cdot \{q + r - 4\} \cdot [p - 2]^2 [q]^2 [q - 1]^1 [r - 1]^1 (P, Q, R)^3 \\
&\quad + 1 \cdot \{q + r - 3\} \cdot \{q + r - 4\} \cdot [q]^3 [r]^3 (P, Q, R)^3.
\end{align*}

From these four equations the law is evident, except as to the numbers subtracted from $q + r$. These are obtained, as explained above, in regard to the numbers added to $a + \beta$ in the $\{\}$ symbols; transforming the diagrams so as to be directly applicable to the case now in question, they become
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cccccccc}
 & 01 & & 1 & & 2 & & 1 \\
 & 1 & & 2 & & 11 & & 2 \\
01 & & 012 & & 012 & & 0123 & \\
 & 0123 & & 03 & & 3 & & 015 \\
 & 4 & & 0127 & & 14 & & 21 \\
 & 036 & & 31 & & 0158 & & 34 \\
 & 12 & & 156 & & 22 & & 0378 \\
3 & & 456 & & 13 & & 4 & 1678 \\
 & & & & & & & 5678
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
showing how the numbers are obtained for the equations 2, 3, 4, 5 respectively.

The first equation is
\[
\{q + r\} Q(P, R) = [p]^1 [q]^1 P(Q, R) + [q]^1 [r]^1 [Q(P, R) + R(P, Q)],
\]
viz. this is
\[
= [p]^1 P(Q, R) - [q]^1 Q(R, P) + [r]^1 R(P, Q) + (q^2 + qr) Q(R, P);
\]
or, dividing by $q$, this is
\[
= p P(Q, R) + q Q(R, P) + r R(P, Q),
\]
which is a well-known identity.

We may verify any of the equations, though the process is rather laborious, for the particular values
\[
P = x^{\frac{1}{2}(p+\alpha)} y^{\frac{1}{2}(p-\alpha)}, \quad Q = x^{\frac{1}{2}(q+\beta)} y^{\frac{1}{2}(q-\beta)}, \quad R = x^{\frac{1}{2}(r+\gamma)} y^{\frac{1}{2}(r-\gamma)}.
\]
Thus, taking the second equation, we have, omitting common factors,
\begin{align*}
(Q, R)^2 &= \tfrac{1}{4}(q + \beta)(q + \beta - 2)(r - \gamma)(r - \gamma - 2) \\
&\quad - 2 \cdot \tfrac{1}{4}(q + \beta)(q - \beta)(r + \gamma)(r - \gamma) \\
&\quad + \tfrac{1}{4}(q - \beta)(q - \beta - 2)(r + \gamma)(r + \gamma - 2) \\
&= \beta^2(r^2 - r) + \gamma^2(q^2 - q) - 2\beta\gamma(q - 1)(r - 1) - qr(q + r - 2),
\end{align*}
\begin{multline*}
(P, (Q, R)^2)^2 = (q + \beta)(r - \gamma) \cdot \tfrac{1}{4}(p + \alpha)(q + r - \beta - \gamma - 2) \\
- \tfrac{1}{4}(p - \alpha)(q + r + \beta + \gamma - 2) \\
= (\beta r - \gamma q)(\alpha(q + r - 2) - p(\beta + \gamma)) \\
= \alpha\beta r(r + q - 2) - \alpha\gamma q(q + r - 2) - p\beta r^2 + p(\beta - r)\beta\gamma + pq\gamma^2,
\end{multline*}
and from the first of these the expressions of $Q(P, R)^2$ and $(P, QR)^2$ are at once obtained.

The identity to be verified then becomes
\begin{multline*}
[q + r]^3 [q]^2 \{\alpha^2(\beta^2(r^2 - r) + \gamma^2(q^2 - q) - 2\beta\gamma(q - 1)(r - 1) - pr(p + r - 2))\} = \\
\{q + r\} [q]^2 [p]^2 \{\beta^2(\gamma^2(r^2 - r) + \alpha^2(p^2 - p) - 2\gamma\alpha(p - 1)(r - 1) - qr(q + r - 2))\} \\
+ 2\{q + r - 1\} [q]^2 (p - 1)(r - 1) \{\alpha\beta\gamma r(q + r - 2) - \alpha\gamma q(q + r - 2) \\
- p\beta\gamma^2 + p(q - r)\beta\gamma + pq\gamma^2\} \\
+ \{q + r - 2\} [q]^2 [r]^2 \{\alpha^2(q + r)(q + r - 1) + (\beta + \gamma)^2(p^2 - p) \\
- 2\alpha(\beta + \gamma)(p - 1)(q + r - 1) - p(q + r)(p + q + r - 2)\},
\end{multline*}
which is easily verified, term by term; for instance, the terms with $\alpha$, $\beta$, or $\gamma$, give
\begin{multline*}
[q + r]^3 [q]^2 pr(p + r - 2) = (q + r) [q]^2 [p]^2 qr(q + r - 2) \\
+ \{q + r - 2\} [q]^2 [r]^2 p(q + r)(p + q + r - 2),
\end{multline*}
which, omitting the factor $(q + r)(q + r - 2)[q]^2 pr$, is
\[
(q + r - 1)(p + r - 2) = (p - 1)q + (r - 1)(p + q + r + 2);
\]
viz. the right-hand side is
\[
(p - 1)q + (r - 1)q + (r - 1)(p + r - 2) = (q + r - 1)(p + r - 2),
\]
as it should be.

The equations are useful for the demonstration of a subsidiary theorem employed in Gordan's demonstration of the finite number of the covariants of any binary form $V$. Suppose that a system of covariants (including the quantic itself) is $P, Q, R, S, \ldots$; this may be the complete system of covariants; and if it is so, then, $T$ and $V$ being any functions of the form $P^\alpha Q^\beta R^\gamma \ldots$, every derivative $(T, V)^\theta$ must be a term or sum of terms of the like form $P^\alpha Q^\beta R^\gamma \ldots$; the subsidiary theorem is that in order to prove that the case is so, it is sufficient to prove that every derivative $(P, Q)^\theta$, where $P$ and $Q$ are any two terms of the proposed system, is a term or sum of terms of the form in question $P^\alpha Q^\beta R^\gamma \ldots$.

In fact, supposing it shown that every derivative $(T, V)^\theta$ up to a given value $\theta$ of is of the form $P^\alpha Q^\beta R^\gamma \ldots$, we can by successive application of the equation for $Q(P, R)^{\theta + 1}$ regarded as an equation for the reduction of the last term on the right-hand side $(P, QR)^{\theta + 1}$, bring first $(P, QR)^{\theta + 1}$ and then $(P, QRS)^{\theta + 1}$, \&c., and so ultimately any function $(P, V)^{\theta + 1}$, and then again any functions $(PQ, V)^{\theta + 1}$, $(PQR, V)^{\theta + 1}$, \&c., and so ultimately any function $(T, V)^{\theta + 1}$ into the required form $P^\alpha Q^\beta R^\gamma \ldots$: or the theorem, being true for $\theta$, will be true for $\theta + 1$; whence it is true generally.
\hfill C.~X. 36

\newpage

\section*{682. Formulae Relating to the Right Line.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{682. Formulae Relating to the Right Line}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 169---171.]

1. Let $\lambda$, $\mu$, $\nu$ be the direction-angles of a line; $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$ the coordinates of a point on the line; and write
\begin{align*}
a &= \cos \lambda, & f &= \beta \cos \nu - \gamma \cos \mu, \\
b &= \cos \mu, & g &= \gamma \cos \lambda - \alpha \cos \nu, \\
c &= \cos \nu, & h &= \alpha \cos \mu - \beta \cos \lambda,
\end{align*}
whence
\[
a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = 1, \quad af + bg + ch = 0,
\]
or the six quantities $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$, termed the coordinates of the line, depend upon four arbitrary parameters.

2. It is at once shown that the condition for the intersection of any two lines $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$, $(a', b', c', f', g', h')$, is
\[
af' + bg' + ch' + a'f + b'g + c'h = 0.
\]

3. Given two lines $(a, b, c, f, g, h)$, $(a', b', c', f', g', h')$, it is required to find their shortest distance, and the coordinates of their line of shortest distance.

Let
\[
Ax + By + Cz + D = 0, \quad Ax + By + Cz + D' = 0,
\]
be parallel planes containing the two lines respectively; then the first plane contains the point $\alpha + r\cos\lambda$, $\beta + r\cos\mu$, $\gamma + r\cos\nu$, and the second contains the point $\alpha' + r'\cos\lambda'$, $\beta' + r'\cos\mu'$, $\gamma' + r'\cos\nu'$; that is, we have
\begin{align*}
A\alpha + B\beta + C\gamma + D &= 0, \\
A\alpha' + B\beta' + C\gamma' + D' &= 0, \\
A\cos\lambda + B\cos\mu + C\cos\nu &= 0, \\
A\cos\lambda' + B\cos\mu' + C\cos\nu' &= 0,
\end{align*}
which last equations may be written
\[
Aa + Bb + Cc = 0, \quad Aa' + Bb' + Cc' = 0,
\]
giving
\[
A : B : C = bc' - b'c : ca' - c'a : ab' - a'b,
\]
or, if we write
\[
\Theta = aa' + bb' + cc',
\]
and assume, as is convenient,
\[
A^2 + B^2 + C^2 = 1,
\]
then
\[
A, B, C = \frac{bc' - b'c, \quad ca' - c'a, \quad ab' - a'b}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)},
\]
where $\Theta = \text{cosine-inclination} = aa' + bb' + cc'$.

Hence, shortest distance $= D' - D$
\begin{align*}
&= A(\alpha - \alpha') + B(\beta - \beta') + C(\gamma - \gamma') \\
&= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \{(bc' - b'c)(\alpha - \alpha') + (ca' - c'a)(\beta - \beta') + (ab' - a'b)(\gamma - \gamma')\} \\
&= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \{a'(c\beta - b\gamma) + b'(\alpha\gamma - c\alpha) + c'(b\alpha - a\beta) \\
&\quad + a(c'\beta' - b'\gamma') + b'(a\gamma' - c'\alpha') + c(b'\alpha' - a'\beta')\} \\
&= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} (af' + bg' + ch' + a'f + b'g + c'h), \quad = \mathfrak{B} \text{ suppose}.
\end{align*}

The six coordinates of the line of shortest distance are $A$, $B$, $C$, $F$, $G$, $H$, where $A$, $B$, $C$ denote as before, and $F$, $G$, $H$ are to be determined.

Since the line meets each of the given lines, we have
\begin{align*}
Af + Bg + Ch + Fa + Gb + Hc &= 0, \\
Af' + Bg' + Ch' + Fa' + Gb' + Hc' &= 0,
\end{align*}
and we have also
\[
FA + GB + HC = 0,
\]
which equations give $F$, $G$, $H$.

Multiplying the first equation by $b'C - c'B$, the second by $Bc - Cb$, and the third by $bc' - b'c$, we find
\[
(b'C - c'B)(Af + Bg + Ch) + (Bc - Cb)(Af' + Bg' + Ch') + F \cdot \surd(1 - \Theta^2) = 0.
\]
Here
\begin{align*}
b'C - c'B &= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \{b'(ab' - a'b) - c'(ca' - c'a)\} \\
&= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \{a(a'^2 + b'^2 + c'^2) - a'(aa' + bb' + cc')\} \\
&= \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} (a - a'\Theta),
\end{align*}
and similarly
\[
cB - bG = \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} (a' - a\Theta).
\]

Also, putting for shortness
\[
\Pi = \begin{vmatrix} a, & b, & c \\ a', & b', & c' \\ f, & g, & h \end{vmatrix}, \quad \Pi' = \begin{vmatrix} a, & b, & c \\ a', & b', & c' \\ f', & g', & h' \end{vmatrix},
\]
we have
\[
Af + Bg + Ch = \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \Pi, \quad Af' + Bg' + Ch' = \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \Pi',
\]
and finally, the determinant which multiplies $F$ is
\[
\frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} \{(bc' - b'c)^2 + (ca' - c'a)^2 + (ab' - a'b)^2\} = \frac{1}{\surd(1 - \Theta^2)} (1 - \Theta^2), \quad = \surd(1 - \Theta^2).
\]

We have thus the value of $F$; forming in the same way those of $G$ and $H$, we find
\begin{align*}
F &= \frac{-1}{(1 - \Theta^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \{(a - a'\Theta)\Pi + (a' - a\Theta)\Pi'\}, \\
G &= \frac{-1}{(1 - \Theta^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \{(b - b'\Theta)\Pi + (b' - b\Theta)\Pi'\}, \\
H &= \frac{-1}{(1 - \Theta^2)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \{(c - c'\Theta)\Pi + (c' - c\Theta)\Pi'\},
\end{align*}
which, with the foregoing equations for $A$, $B$, $C$, give the six coordinates of the line of shortest distance.
\hfill C.~X. 37

\newpage

\section*{683. On the Function $\arcsin(x + iy)$.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{683. On the Function $\arcsin(x + iy)$}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 171---174.]

The determination of the function in question, the arc to a given imaginary sine, is considered in Cauchy's \textit{Exercises d'Analyse}, \&c., t.\ iii.\ (1844), p.\ 382; but it appears, by two hydrodynamical papers by Mr Ferrers and Mr Lamb, \textit{Quarterly Mathematical Journal}, t.\ xiii.\ (1874), p.\ 115, and t.\ xiv.\ (1875), p.\ 40, that the question is connected with the theory of confocal conics.

Taking $c = \surd(a^2 - b^2)$ a positive real quantity which may ultimately be put $= 1$, the question is to find the real quantities $\xi$, $\eta$, such that
\[
\xi + i\eta = \arcsin \tfrac{1}{c}(x + iy),
\]
or say
\[
x + iy = c \sin(\xi + i\eta),
\]
so that
\[
x = c \sin \xi \cos i\eta, \quad iy = c \cos \xi \sin i\eta.
\]

It is convenient to remark that if a value of $\xi + i\eta$ be $\xi' + i\eta'$, then the general value is $2m\pi + \xi' + i\eta'$ or $(2m + 1)\pi - (\xi' + i\eta')$; hence, $\eta$ may be made positive or negative at pleasure; $\cos i\eta$ is in each case positive, but $-\sin i\eta$ has the same sign as $\eta$; hence $\cos \xi$ has the same sign as $x$, but $\sin \xi$ has the same sign as $y$ or the reverse sign, according as $\eta$ is positive or negative; for any given values of $x$ and $y$, we obtain, as will appear, determinate positive values of $\sin^2 \xi$ and $\cos^2 \xi$; and the square roots of these must therefore be taken so as to give to $\sin \xi$, $\cos \xi$ their proper signs respectively.

Suppose that $\lambda$, $\mu$ are the elliptic coordinates of the point $(x, y)$; viz. that we have
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2 + \lambda} + \frac{y^2}{b^2 + \lambda} = 1, \quad \frac{x^2}{a^2 + \mu} + \frac{y^2}{b^2 + \mu} = 1,
\]
where $a^2 + \lambda$, $b^2 + \lambda$, and $a^2 + \mu$ are positive, but $b^2 + \mu$ is negative.

Calling $\rho$, $\sigma$ the distances of the point $(x, y)$ from the points $(c, 0)$ and $(-c, 0)$, that is, assuming
\[
\rho = \surd\{(x - c)^2 + y^2\}, \quad \sigma = \surd\{(x + c)^2 + y^2\},
\]
then we have
\begin{align*}
\surd(a^2 + \lambda) &= \tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma + \rho), & \text{whence also } \surd(b^2 + \lambda) &= \tfrac{1}{2} \surd\{(\sigma + \rho)^2 - 4c^2\}, \\
\surd(a^2 + \mu) &= \tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma - \rho), & \surd(b^2 + \mu) &= \tfrac{1}{2} \surd\{(\sigma - \rho)^2 - 4c^2\},
\end{align*}
which equations determine $\lambda$, $\mu$ as functions of $x$, $y$.

Now we have
\[
\rho\sigma = \surd\{(a^2 + \lambda)(a^2 + \mu) - c^2\} = \surd\{(x^2 - y^2 - c^2)^2 + 4x^2y^2\},
\]
\[
\rho^2 + \sigma^2 = 2(x^2 + y^2 + c^2);
\]
substituting herein for $x$, $y$ their values $c \sin \xi \cos i\eta$, $-ci \cos \xi \sin i\eta$, we find
\begin{align*}
x^2 - y^2 - c^2 &= c^2(\sin^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta + \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta) - c^2(\sin^2 \xi + \cos^2 \xi)(\sin^2 i\eta + \cos^2 i\eta) \\
&= -c^2(\sin^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta + \cos^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta),
\end{align*}
whence
\begin{align*}
(x^2 - y^2 - c^2)^2 &= c^4(\cos^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta + \sin^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta)^2, \\
4x^2y^2 &= -4c^4 \sin^2 \xi \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta \cos^2 i\eta \\
&= c^4(\cos^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta - \sin^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta)^2.
\end{align*}
Hence
\[
2\rho\sigma = 2c^2(\cos^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta - \sin^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta),
\]
and
\[
\rho^2 + \sigma^2 = 2c^2(\sin^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta + \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta + 1);
\]
hence
\[
(\rho + \sigma)^2 = 2c^2(\cos^2 i\eta - \sin^2 i\eta + 1) = 4c^2 \cos^2 i\eta,
\]
\[
(\rho - \sigma)^2 = 2c^2(\sin^2 \xi - \cos^2 \xi + 1) = 4c^2 \sin^2 \xi.
\]
Consequently
\[
a^2 + \lambda = c^2 \cos^2 i\eta, \text{ and thence } b^2 + \lambda = -c^2 \sin^2 i\eta,
\]
\[
a^2 + \mu = c^2 \sin^2 \xi, \text{ and } b^2 + \mu = -c^2 \cos^2 \xi,
\]
values which verify as they should do the equations
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2 + \lambda} + \frac{y^2}{b^2 + \lambda} = 1, \quad \frac{x^2}{a^2 + \mu} + \frac{y^2}{b^2 + \mu} = 1;
\]
viz. these become
\[
\frac{c^2 \sin^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta}{c^2 \cos^2 i\eta} + \frac{-c^2 \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta}{-c^2 \sin^2 i\eta} = \sin^2 \xi + \cos^2 \xi = 1,
\]
\[
\frac{c^2 \sin^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta}{c^2 \sin^2 \xi} + \frac{-c^2 \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta}{-c^2 \cos^2 \xi} = \cos^2 i\eta + \sin^2 i\eta = 1.
\]

The same equations, or as we may also write them,
\[
\lambda = -a^2 \sin^2 i\eta - b^2 \cos^2 i\eta, \quad \mu = -a^2 \cos^2 \xi - b^2 \sin^2 \xi,
\]
determine $\eta$ as a function of $\lambda$, and $\xi$ as a function of $\mu$; $\lambda$, $\mu$ being by what precedes, given functions of $x$, $y$.

Or more simply, starting from the last-mentioned values of $\lambda$, $\mu$, and substituting these in the expressions
\[
\xi = \frac{a^2 + \lambda}{a^2 - b^2}, \quad \eta = \frac{b^2 + \lambda}{b^2 - a^2},
\]
we find
\[
x^2 = c^2 \sin^2 \xi \cos^2 i\eta, \quad y^2 = -c^2 \cos^2 \xi \sin^2 i\eta,
\]
or say
\[
x = c \sin \xi \cos i\eta, \quad iy = c \cos \xi \sin i\eta,
\]
whence
\[
x + iy = c \sin(\xi + i\eta),
\]
the original relation between $x$, $y$ and $\xi$, $\eta$.
\hfill $37\text{---}2$

\newpage

\section*{684. On a Relation between Certain Products of Differences.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{684. On a Relation between Certain Products of Differences}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 174, 175.]

Consider the function
\[
3\left\{\begin{array}{l}
abc \cdot de \\
+ bcd \cdot ea \\
+ cde \cdot ab \\
+ dea \cdot bc \\
+ eab \cdot cd
\end{array}\right\}
-
\left\{\begin{array}{l}
abd \cdot ce \\
+ bce \cdot da \\
+ cda \cdot eb \\
+ deb \cdot ac \\
+ eac \cdot bd
\end{array}\right\},
\]
where
\[
abc = (a - b)(b - c)(c - a), \quad ab = (a - b)(b - a) = -(a - b)^2, \quad \text{\&c.},
\]
therefore
\[
abc = bca = cab = -bac, \text{ \&c.}, \quad ab = ba.
\]

It is to be shown that the function vanishes if $e = d$. Writing $e = d$, the value is
\[
3(bcd \cdot da + dab \cdot cd) - abd \cdot cd - bcd \cdot da - cda \cdot db - dac \cdot bd,
\]
viz. this is
\begin{align*}
&3\, bcd \cdot ad - abd \cdot cd + 3\, abd \cdot cd - bcd \cdot ad - 2acd \cdot bd \\
&= 2\, bcd \cdot ad - 2acd \cdot bd + 2abd \cdot cd \\
&= 2(bcd \cdot ad + cad \cdot bd + abd \cdot cd),
\end{align*}
which is easily seen to vanish; the value is
\begin{align*}
&(b - c)(c - a)(d - b)(a - d)^2 = -(b - c)(a - d)^2(b - d)(c - d) \\
&+ (c - a)(a - d)(d - e)(b - d)^2 - (c - a)(a - d)(b - d)^2(c - d) \\
&+ (a - b)(b - d)(d - a)(c - d)^2 - (a - b)(a - d)(b - d)(c - d)^2;
\end{align*}
viz. omitting the factor $(a - d)(b - d)(c - d)$, this is
\[
= -(b - c)(a - d) - (c - a)(b - d) - (a - b)(c - d),
\]
which vanishes.

Hence the function also vanishes if $e = a$, or $a = b$ or $b = c$, or $c = d$; and it is thus a mere numerical multiple of $(a - b)(b - c)(c - d)(d - e)(e - a)$, or say it is $= M \cdot abcde$.

To find $M$ write $e = c$, the equation becomes
\begin{align*}
&3abc \cdot dc - cda \cdot cb = M \cdot abc \cdot dc, \\
&+ 3bcd \cdot ca - acd \cdot bc \\
&+ 3cda \cdot bc + 3cab \cdot cd,
\end{align*}
\[
4abc \cdot dc + 4dbc \cdot ac + 4adc \cdot bc = M \cdot abc \cdot dc,
\]
giving $M = 10$.

In fact, we then have
\[
-4abc \cdot dc + 4dbc \cdot ac + 4adc \cdot bc = 0,
\]
that is,
\[
-abc \cdot dc - bde \cdot ac - dac \cdot bc = 0,
\]
which is right.

And we have thus the identity
\[
3\left\{\begin{array}{l}
abc \cdot de \\
+ bcd \cdot ea \\
+ cde \cdot ab \\
+ dea \cdot bc \\
+ eab \cdot cd
\end{array}\right\}
-
\left\{\begin{array}{l}
abd \cdot ce \\
+ bce \cdot da \\
+ cda \cdot eb \\
+ deb \cdot ac \\
+ eac \cdot bd
\end{array}\right\}
= 10 \cdot abcde,
\]
viz. this is
\[
3[abcde] - [acebd] = 10\{abcde\}.
\]

\newpage

\section*{685. On Mr Cotterill's Goniometrical Problem.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{685. On Mr Cotterill's Goniometrical Problem}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 196---198.]

The very remarkable formulae contained in Mr Cotterill's paper, ``A goniometrical problem, to be solved analytically in one move, or more simply synthetically in two moves,'' \textit{Quarterly Mathematical Journal}, t.\ vii.\ (1866), pp.\ 259---272, are presented in a form which, to say the least, is not as easily intelligible as might be; and they have not, I think, attracted the attention which they well deserve.

Using his notation, except that I write for angles small roman letters, in order to be able to have the corresponding italic small letters and capitals for the sines and cosines respectively of the same angles, we consider nine angles $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$, $e$, $f$, $x$, $y$, $z$, which are such that the sum of three angles in the same line, or in the same column, is an odd multiple of $\pi$.

Of course, any four angles such as $a$, $b$, $d$, $e$ are arbitrary, and each of the remaining angles is then determinate save as to an even multiple of $\pi$. And it may be remarked that these angles $a$, $b$, $d$, $e$ may represent the inclinations of any four lines to a fifth line, and that the remaining angles are then at once obtained, as in the figure.

The small roman letters are here used to denote as well angles as points, being so placed as to show what the angles are which they respectively denote; the points $*$, $*$ are constructed as the intersections of the lines $ac$, $bc$ by the circle circumscribed about $fxy$, and the angle $z$ is the angle which the points $*$, $*$ subtend at $x$ or $y$. It will be observed that the sum of the three angles in a line or column is in each case $= \pi$.

But this in passing: the analytical theorem is, first, we can form with the sines and cosines of the angles in any two lines or columns a function $\mathfrak{S}$ presenting itself under two distinct forms, which are in fact equal in value, or say $\mathfrak{S}$ is a symmetrical function of the two lines or columns, viz. for the first and second lines this is
\[
\mathfrak{S}\binom{a,b,c}{d,e,f} = a^2 A\mathfrak{h}c + b^2 Bca + f^2 C\mathfrak{a}b = a'^2 D\mathfrak{e}f + b'^2 E\mathfrak{f}d + c'^2 F\mathfrak{d}e,
\]
where, as already mentioned, $a$, $A$ denote $\sin a$, $\cos a$, and so for the other letters.

Secondly, if to the $\mathfrak{S}$ of any two lines or columns we add twice the product of the six sines, we obtain a sum $\mathfrak{M}$ which has the same value from whichever two lines or columns we obtain it; or, say $\mathfrak{M}$ is a symmetrical function of the matrix of the nine angles.

Thus
\[
\mathfrak{M} = \mathfrak{S}\binom{a,b,c}{d,e,f} + 2abcdef;
\]
which is one of a system of six forms each of which (on account of the two forms of the $\mathfrak{S}$ contained in it) may be regarded as a double form, and the twelve values are all of them equal.

There are, moreover, 15 other forms of $\mathfrak{M}$, viz. 3 line-forms, such as $bcdx + caey + abfz$ (belongs to line $a$, $b$, $c$), 3 column-forms, such as $dxbc + xaef + adyz$ (belongs to column $a$, $d$, $x$), and 9 term-forms, such as $e\beta\gamma x^2 + f\gamma\alpha y^2 + 2efyz A$ (belongs to term $a$), and the $12 + 15 = 27$ values are all equal.

The several identities can of course be verified by means of the relations between the nine angles, or rather the derived sine- and cosine-relations
\[
c = aB + bA, \quad C = ab - AB, \quad \text{\&c.}
\]

As regards the two forms of $\mathfrak{S}$, the identity to be verified may be written
\[
c(a'^2 De + b'^2 Ed - c'^2 F\mathfrak{a}b) = f(a'^2 De + b'^2 Ed - f^2 C\mathfrak{a}b).
\]
Proceeding to reduce the factor $a'^2 De + b'^2 Ed - f^2 C\mathfrak{a}b$, if we first write herein $f = eD + dE$, it becomes
\[
a'^2 De + b'^2 Ed - (eD + dE)C\mathfrak{a}b,
\]
which is
\[
= aDe(a - bC) + bEd(b - aC),
\]
and then writing $C = ab - AB$, we have $a - bC = a(1 - b^2) + bAB = B(aB + bA) = Bc$; and, similarly, $b - aC = Ac$; whence the term is $= c(aeBD + bdAE)$; or, in the equation to be verified, the right-hand side is $= cf(aeBD + bdAE)$, and by a similar reduction, the left-hand side is found to have the same value.

The paper contains various other interesting results.
\hfill C.~X. 38

\newpage

\section*{686. On a Functional Equation.}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{686. On a Functional Equation}

[From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ xv.\ (1878), pp.\ 315---325; \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, vol.\ ix.\ (1878), p.\ 29.]

I was led by a hydrodynamical problem to consider a certain functional equation: viz. writing for shortness
\[
x_1 = \frac{-1}{cx + d},
\]
this is
\[
\phi x_1 - \phi x = \frac{Ax + B}{Cx + D}.
\]

I find by a direct process, which I will afterwards explain, the solution
\[
\phi x = \frac{A}{\surd\{(a - d)^2 + 4bc\}} \cdot \frac{(AD - BC)}{c(cC - cD)} \int \frac{\sin \xi t \sin \eta t \, dt}{\sin \zeta t \sinh \pi t},
\]
where $\xi$ is a constant, but $\xi$, $\eta$ are complicated logarithmic functions of $x$ (which depend also on the quantities $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$, $C$, $D$); $\sinh \pi t$ denotes as usual the hyperbolic sine, $\tfrac{1}{2}(e^{\pi t} - e^{-\pi t})$.

The values of $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$ are given by the formulae
\[
\cos \zeta = \frac{a + d}{\surd\{(a + d)^2 - 4(ad - bc)\}} = \frac{a + d}{\surd\{(a - d)^2 + 4bc\}},
\]
\begin{align*}
\mathfrak{a} &= ax + b, & \mathfrak{h} &= -dx + b, & \mathfrak{c} &= cx + d, & \mathfrak{d} &= cx - a, \\
W &= C\mathfrak{a} + B\mathfrak{c}, & Z &= C\mathfrak{h} + D\mathfrak{d}, & \\
R &= W + \lambda Z, & S &= -W - \lambda Z, & \\
R' &= W + \lambda' Z, & S' &= -W - \lambda' Z, &
\end{align*}
which determine $\lambda$, $R$, $S$, $R'$, $S'$ and then
\[
\xi = \tfrac{1}{2} \log \frac{RS'}{R'S}, \quad \eta = \tfrac{1}{2} \left(\log \lambda + \log \frac{RR'}{SS'}\right), \quad \zeta = \tfrac{1}{2} \log \lambda.
\]

There is some difficulty as to the definite integral, on account of the denominator factor $\sin \zeta t$, which becomes $= 0$ for the series of values $t = \frac{m\pi}{\zeta}$, but this is a point which I do not enter into.

I will in the first instance verify the result. Writing $x_1$ in place of $x$, and taking $\xi_1$, $\eta_1$ to denote the corresponding values of $\xi$, $\eta$, it will be shown that
\[
\xi_1 = \xi, \quad \eta_1 = \eta + 2\zeta,
\]
see post, (1).

Hence in the difference $\phi x - \phi x_1$ we have the integral
\[
\int \frac{\sin \zeta t \{\sin \eta t - \sin(\eta + 2\zeta)t\} \, dt}{\sin \zeta t \sinh \pi t},
\]
(where and in all that follows the limits are $\infty$, $0$, as before); here, since
\[
\sin \eta t - \sin(\eta + 2\zeta)t = -2\sin \zeta t \cos(\eta + \zeta)t,
\]
the factor $\sin \zeta t$ divides out, and the numerator is $= -2\sin \zeta t \cos(\eta + \zeta)t$, which is
\[
= \sin(\eta + \zeta - \xi)t - \sin(\eta + \zeta + \xi)t.
\]
Hence the integral in question is
\[
\int \frac{\sin(\eta + \zeta - \xi)t \, dt}{\sinh \pi t} - \int \frac{\sin(\eta + \zeta + \xi)t \, dt}{\sinh \pi t}.
\]

Now we have in general
\[
\frac{1}{1 + \exp. \alpha} = \tfrac{1}{2} - \int \frac{\sin \alpha t \, dt}{\sinh \pi t};
\]
(this is, in fact, Poisson's formula
\[
\frac{1}{1 + k\beta^{2n}} = \tfrac{1}{2} - 2\int \frac{\sin(2n \log \beta + \log k)t \cdot dt}{e^{\pi t} - e^{-\pi t}},
\]
in the second Memoir on the distribution of Electricity, \&c., M\'{e}m.\ de l'Inst, 1811, p.\ 223); and hence the value is
\[
-\frac{1}{1 + \exp. (\eta + \zeta - \xi)} + \frac{1}{1 + \exp. (\eta + \zeta + \xi)},
\]
or since
\[
\eta + \zeta = \log\lambda + \tfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{RR'}{SS'}, \quad \xi = \tfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{RS'}{R'S},
\]
we have
\[
\eta + \zeta - \xi = \log\lambda + \tfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{R'^2}{S'^2} = \log\lambda \cdot \frac{R'}{S'},
\]
\[
\eta + \zeta + \xi = \log\lambda + \tfrac{1}{2}\log\frac{R^2}{S^2} = \log\lambda \cdot \frac{R}{S}.
\]
\hfill $38\text{---}2$

And the value is thus
\[
= -\frac{1}{1 + \lambda \dfrac{R'}{S'}} + \frac{1}{1 + \lambda \dfrac{R}{S}}, \quad = -\frac{(RS' - R'S)\lambda}{(\lambda R' + S')(\lambda R + S)}.
\]

Hence, from the assumed value of $\phi x$, we obtain
\[
\phi x - \phi x_1 = \frac{A}{C}(x - x_1) - \frac{\surd\{(a - d)^2 + 4bc\}\,(AD - BC)\,(RS' - R'S)\lambda}{C(dC - cD)(\lambda R' + S')(\lambda R + S)}.
\]

We have
\[
RS' - R'S = \frac{(\lambda - 1)(a + d)^2}{ad - bc} (dC - cD) \{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\},
\]
\[
R\lambda + S = (\lambda^2 - 1)(cx + d), \qquad \text{see post, (2),}
\]
\[
R'\lambda + S' = (\lambda - 1)(a + d)(Cx + D),
\]
or since
\[
\frac{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b}{cx + d} = x - x_1,
\]
this is
\[
\phi x - \phi x_1 = \frac{A}{C}(x - x_1) - \frac{\surd\{(a - d)^2 + 4bc\}\,(AD - BC)\,(a + d)\lambda}{(ad - bc)(\lambda^2 - 1)(Cx + D)}(x - x_1).
\]

But from the value of $\lambda$,
\[
\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^2 - 1} = \frac{ad - bc}{(a + d)\surd\{(a - d)^2 + 4bc\}},
\]
and the equation thus is
\[
\phi x - \phi x_1 = (x - x_1)\left\{\frac{A}{C} - \frac{AD - BC}{C(Cx + D)}\right\}, \quad = (x - x_1)\frac{Ax + B}{Cx + D},
\]
as it should be.

(1) For the foregoing values of $\xi_1$, $\eta_1$, we require $R_1$, $S_1$, $R_1'$, $S_1'$, the values which $R$, $S$, $R'$, $S'$ assume on writing therein $x_1$ for $x$. We have
\[
R_1 = \lambda(cx_1 + d) + (cx_1 - a), \quad S_1 = -(cx_1 + d) - \lambda(cx_1 - a):
\]
substituting for $x_1$ its value, we find
\[
R_1(cx + d) = (a + d)\lambda(cx + d) - (ad - bc)(\lambda + 1),
\]
or writing herein
\[
ad - bc = \frac{(a + d)^2\lambda}{(\lambda + 1)^2},
\]
this is
\[
R_1(cx + d) = \frac{(a + d)\lambda}{\lambda + 1} R;
\]
and similarly
\[
S_1(cx + d) = \frac{a + d}{\lambda + 1} S.
\]

\newpage
\clearpage

\newpage

% ============================================================
% PAPER 686: ON A FUNCTIONAL EQUATION
% ============================================================
\section[On a Functional Equation]{686. On a Functional Equation.\footnote{From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ XV. (1878), pp.\ 131--137.}}

[301]

\noindent
[The paper continues from the previous page with the verification of certain formulae.]

For the value of $RS' - R'S$, we have
\begin{align*}
RS' - R'S &= (\lambda c + d)(-W - \lambda Z) - (-\lambda d - c)(W + \lambda Z)\\
&= -(\lambda^2 - 1)\{cZ + (\lambda + 1)((d-c)W - dR\},
\end{align*}
or substituting for $Z$ and $W$ their values, this is
\begin{multline*}
= -\frac{(\lambda - 1)}{\lambda}\bigl[(a^2 + d^2 + ad + bc)c\,(bC + dD)\\
+ (ad - bc)\{(c-d)(aC + cD) + d(bC + dD)\}\bigr].
\end{multline*}

In the term in $\{\dots\}$, the coefficient of $C$ is
\[
[(a^2 + d^2 + ad + bc)b + (ad - bc)a]c - d(a - b)(ad - bc)
= (a + d)(db - bd)c - (a + d)dx(ad - bc),
\]
and similarly the coefficient of $D$ is
\[
[(a^2 + d^2 + ad + bc)d + (ad - bc)c]c - d(c - d)(ad - bc)
= (a + d)(ad - bc)c - (a + d)d(ad - bc).
\]

Hence the whole term in $\{\dots\}$ is
\[
= (a + d)\{[(db - bd)c - d(ad - bc)x]C + [(ad - bc)c - d(ad - bc)]D\},
\]
which is readily reduced to
\[
(a + d)(ad - bc)(-dC + cD);
\]
also
\[
ad - bc = (a + d)\{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\},
\]
so that we have
\[
RS' - R'S = \frac{(\lambda - 1)}{\lambda}(dC - cD)\{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\},
\]
which is the required value of $RS' - R'S$; and there is no difficulty in obtaining the other two formulae,
\[
RX + S = (\lambda^2 - 1)(cx + d),\quad R'\lambda + S' = (\lambda - 1)(a + d)(Cx + D);
\]
the verification is thus completed.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(1)}\quad
To show how the formula was directly obtained, we have
\[
\frac{Ax + B}{Cx + D} = \frac{A}{C} - \frac{AD - BC}{C}\frac{1}{Cx + D}
= \frac{A}{C} + \rho x \quad \text{suppose};
\]
the equation then is
\[
\phi x - \phi x_1 = \frac{A}{C}(x - x_1) + (x - x_1)\rho x.
\]

Hence, if $x_1$, $x_2$, $x_3$, \dots\ denote the successive functions $\phi x$, $\phi^2 x$, $\phi^3 x$, \&c., we have
\begin{align*}
\phi x - \phi x_1 &= \frac{A}{C}(x - x_1) + (x - x_1)\rho x,\\
\phi x_1 - \phi x_2 &= \frac{A}{C}(x_1 - x_2) + (x_1 - x_2)\rho x_1,
\end{align*}
whence adding, and neglecting $\phi x_n$ and $x_n$, we have
\[
\phi x - \frac{A}{C}x = [(x - x_1)\rho x + (x_1 - x_2)\rho x_1 + (x_2 - x_3)\rho x_2 + \dots],
\]
where the term itself a given function of $x$ [therein regarding $x_1$, $x_2$, $x_3$, \dots\ as given functions of $x$], and it only remains to sum the series.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(2)}\quad
Starting from
\[
x_1 = \frac{ax + b}{cx + d},
\]
and writing
\[
\lambda = \frac{a^2 + d^2 + 2bc}{ad - bc},
\]
then the $n$th function is given by the formula
\[
x_n = \frac{(\lambda^{n+1} - 1)(ax + b) + (\lambda^n - \lambda)(-dx + b)}
{(\lambda^{n+1} - 1)c + (\lambda^n - \lambda)d}
= \frac{\lambda^n P + Q}{\lambda^n R + S},
\]
if $P = \lambda a + b$, $Q = -\lambda d + b$, and as before $R = \lambda c + d$, $S = -\lambda c - \lambda d$.

I stop to remark that $\lambda$ being real, if $\lambda > 1$ we have $\lambda^n$ very large, and $x_n = \rho$ which is independent of $n$; the value in question for $n$ very large is
\[
x_{n-1} = x_n = \frac{\lambda(ax + b) + (-dx + b)}{\lambda(cx + d) + (-cx - a)},
\]
which, observing that the equation in $\lambda$ may be written
\[
\frac{\lambda a - d}{c(\lambda + 1)} = \frac{b(\lambda + 1)}{\lambda d - a},
\]
is, in fact, independent of $x$, and calling each of these $= x$, we have $x_{n-1} = x_n$, or if the two equal values $= x$,
\[
x = \frac{ax + b}{cx + a},
\]
which is the same equation as is obtainable by the elimination of $\lambda$ from the equations
\[
x = \frac{\lambda a - d}{c(\lambda + 1)} = \frac{b(\lambda + 1)}{\lambda d - a}.
\]

The same result is obtained by taking $\lambda < 1$ and consequently $x_n = \frac{b}{d}$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(3)}\quad
We find
\[
x_{n-1} - x_n = \frac{(\lambda^n P + Q)(\lambda^{n-1}R + S) - (\lambda^{n-1}P + Q)(\lambda^n R + S)}
{(\lambda^{n-1}R + S)(\lambda^n R + S)},
\]
where
\[
PS - QR = -(\lambda^2 - 1)(ad - bc) = -(\lambda^2 - 1)(a + d)\{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\},
\]
and therefore
\[
x_{n-1} - x_n = \frac{(\lambda - 1)(\lambda^2 - 1)(a + d)\{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\}\lambda^{n-1}}
{(\lambda^{n-1}R + S)(\lambda^n R + S)}.
\]

Also
\[
\rho x_{n-1} = \frac{AD - BC}{C}\frac{1}{Cx_{n-1} + D}
= \frac{AD - BC}{C}\frac{\lambda^{n-1}R + S}{\lambda^{n-1}W + Z},
\]
where
\[
\frac{W_1}{Z_1} = \frac{C(\lambda^{n-1}P + Q) + D(\lambda^{n-1}R + S)}{\dots},
\]
viz.
\[
W_1 = C(\lambda^{n-1}P + Q) + D(\lambda^{n-1}R + S),\quad Z_1 = C(-\lambda d - c)Q + D(\dots)
\]
[Further algebraic manipulations continue.]

We hence obtain
\[
\phi x - \frac{A}{C}x = \frac{(\lambda - 1)(\lambda^2 - 1)(a + d)\{cx^2 + (d - a)x - b\}}
{\lambda}\frac{(AD - BC)}{C(cD - dC)}
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty} \frac{(RS' - R'S)\lambda^n}{(\lambda^{n-1}R + S)(\lambda^n R + S)}.
\]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{(4)}\quad
Now the before-mentioned integral formula gives
\[
\frac{1}{\lambda^n} = \frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin(n\log\lambda + \log k)\,t}{\sinh\pi t}\,dt,
\]
and
\[
\frac{1}{\lambda'^n} = \frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin(n\log\lambda + \log k')\,t}{\sinh\pi t}\,dt.
\]

Taking the difference, and then writing $k = -\frac{S'}{R'}$, $k' = -\frac{S}{R}$, we have under the integral sign
\[
\sin\bigl(n\log\lambda + \log(-\tfrac{S'}{R'})\bigr)t - \sin\bigl(n\log\lambda + \log(-\tfrac{S}{R})\bigr)t,
\]
which is
\[
= 2\sin\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(\log\tfrac{RS'}{R'S}\bigr)t\,\cos\bigl(n\log\lambda + \tfrac{1}{2}\log\tfrac{RS'}{R'S}\bigr)t,
\]
which attending to the before-mentioned values of $\xi$, $\eta$, $\zeta$ is
\[
= 2\sin\xi t\,\cos(2n\xi - \zeta + \eta)t,
\]
and the formula thus is
\[
\frac{(RS' - R'S)\lambda^n}{(R\lambda^n + S)(R'\lambda^n + S')}
= \frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin\xi t\,\cos(2n\xi - \zeta + \eta)t}{\sinh\pi t}\,dt.
\]

We have here
\[
\cos(2n\xi - \zeta + \eta)t = \cos 2n\xi t\,\cos(\eta - \zeta)t - \sin 2n\xi t\,\sin(\eta - \zeta)t,
\]
whence summing from $1$ to $\infty$, by means of the formulae
\[
\cos 2\xi t + \cos 4\xi t + \dots = -\tfrac{1}{2},\qquad
\sin 2\xi t + \sin 4\xi t + \dots = \tfrac{1}{2}\cot\xi t,
\]
(which series however are not convergent), the numerator under the integral sign becomes
\[
\sin\xi t\{-\cos(\eta - \zeta)t - \cot\xi t\,\sin(\eta - \zeta)t\},
\]
which is
\[
-\frac{\sin\eta t}{\sin\xi t},
\]
and the formula thus is
\[
\frac{(RS' - R'S)\lambda^n}{(R\lambda^n + S)(R'\lambda^n + S')}
= -\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin\eta t}{\sin\xi t\,\sinh\pi t}\,dt,
\]
and we therefore find
\[
\phi x = \frac{A}{C}x + \frac{\sqrt{(a-d)^2 + 4bc}\,(AD - BC)}{C(cD - dC)}
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \frac{\sin\xi t\,\sin\eta t\,dt}{\sin\xi t\,\sinh\pi t},
\]
which is the result in question.

The general solution is one particular solution $+$ the general solution of $\phi x = \frac{A}{C}x$; calling it $\phi x$, we have
\[
\phi x = \frac{A}{C}x + K\frac{Ax + B}{Cx + D},
\]
where $K$ is an arbitrary constant.

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 687: NOTE ON THE FUNCTION
% ============================================================
\section[Note on the function $\phi x = a^x(c-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\{c(c-x)-b^2\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}$]{687. Note on the function $\phi x = a^x(c-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\{c(c-x)-b^2\}^{-\frac{1}{2}}$.\footnote{From the \textit{Messenger of Mathematics}, vol.\ VII. (1878), pp.\ 113--114.}}

[307]

Starting from the general form
\[
\phi x = \frac{ax + b}{cx + d},
\]
we have
\[
x_n = \frac{(\lambda^{n+1} - 1)(ax + \beta) + (\lambda^n - \lambda)(-\delta x + \beta)}
{(\lambda^{n+1} - 1)(\gamma x + \delta) + (\lambda^n - \lambda)(\gamma x - \alpha)},
\]
where
\[
\lambda + \frac{1}{\lambda} = \frac{\alpha^2 + \delta^2 + 2\beta\gamma}{\alpha\delta - \beta\gamma}.
\]

For the function in question
\[
\phi x = a^2(c - x)\sqrt{c(c-x) - b^2}
\]
(form which presents itself in the problem of the distribution of electricity upon two spheres), the values of $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$ are
\[
\alpha = -a^2,\quad \beta = c^2 - b^2,\quad \gamma = -c,\quad \delta = c^2,
\]
the equation for $\lambda$ therefore is
\[
\lambda + \frac{1}{\lambda} = \frac{a^4 + (c^2 - b^2)^2 - 2a^2c^2}{-a^2c^2 + c^2(c^2 - b^2)},
\]
or, what is the same thing,
\[
\frac{(\lambda + 1)^2}{\lambda} = \frac{(a^2 + c^2 - b^2)^2}{c^2(c^2 - b^2)},
\]

[308]

Suppose that $a$, $b$, $c$ are the sides of a triangle the angles whereof are $A$, $B$, $C$; then
\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2 - 2ab\cos C,\quad \text{or we have}\quad
\frac{c^2 - a^2 - b^2}{ab} = -2\cos C;
\]
or, writing this under the form
\[
\sqrt{\lambda} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}} = 2\cos C,
\]
the value of $\lambda$ is at once seen to be $= e^{2iC}$; and it is interesting to obtain the expression of the $n$th function in terms of the sides and angles of the triangle.

The numerator and the denominator are
\[
\lambda^n P + Q,\quad \lambda^n R + S,
\]
where
\begin{align*}
P &= \lambda(\alpha x + \beta) + (-\delta x + \beta),\qquad
Q = -(\alpha x + \beta) - \lambda(-\delta x + \beta),\\
R &= \lambda(\gamma x + \delta) + (\gamma x - \alpha),\qquad
S = -(\gamma x + \delta) - \lambda(\gamma x - \alpha).
\end{align*}

Hence, writing the numerator and the denominator in the forms
\[
\lambda^n P + \lambda^{-n}Q,\quad \lambda^n R + \lambda^{-n}S,
\]
these are
\[
(P + Q)\cos nC + (P - Q)i\sin nC,\quad (R + S)\cos nC + (R - S)i\sin nC;
\]
viz.\ they are
\begin{align*}
&(\lambda - 1)(\alpha + \delta)x\cos nC + (\lambda + 1)\{(\alpha - \delta)x + 2\beta\}i\sin nC,\\
&(\lambda - 1)(\alpha + \delta)\cos nC + (\lambda + 1)\{2\gamma x - (\alpha - \delta)\}i\sin nC,
\end{align*}
or, observing that $\frac{\lambda - 1}{\lambda + 1} = i\tan C$ and removing the common factor $i(\lambda + 1)$, they may be written
\begin{align*}
&\tan C\,(\alpha + \delta)x\cos nC + \{ (\alpha - \delta)x + 2\beta \}\sin nC,\\
&\tan C\,(\alpha + \delta)\cos nC + \{ 2\gamma x - (\alpha - \delta) \}\sin nC.
\end{align*}

Substituting for $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$ their values, these are
\begin{align*}
&\tan C\{(c^2 - a^2 - b^2)x\cos nC\} + \{(b^2 - a^2 - c^2)x + 2a^2c\}\sin nC,\\
&\tan C\{(c^2 - a^2 - b^2)\cos nC\} + \{-2cx - (b^2 - a^2 - c^2)\}\sin nC,
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
&= \tan C\{-ab\cos C\,x\cos nC\} + \{-ac\cos B\,x + a^2c\}\sin nC,\\
&= \tan C\{-ab\cos C\,x\cos nC\} + \{-cx + ac\cos B\}\sin nC,
\end{align*}
\begin{align*}
&= x\{-ab\sin C\cos nC - ac\cos B\sin nC\} + a^2c\sin nC,\\
&\quad -cx\sin nC + \{ac\cos B\sin nC - ab\sin C\cos nC\};
\end{align*}

[309]

or, writing herein $b\sin C = c\sin B$, these are
\begin{align*}
&-acx\{\sin B\cos nC + \cos B\sin nC\} + a^2c\sin nC,\\
&-cx\sin nC + ac\{\cos B\sin nC - \sin B\cos nC\},
\end{align*}
whence finally
\[
\phi^n x = \frac{a^2\sin nC - cx\sin(nC + B)}{a\sin(nC - B) - x\sin nC}.
\]

As a verification, writing $n = 1$, we have
\[
\phi x = \frac{a^2\sin C - cx\sin(C + B)}{a\sin(C - B) - x\sin C}
= \frac{a^2c - acx\frac{\sin A}{\sin C}}{a\sin(C - B) - x\sin C},
\]
or observing that
\[
\frac{ac}{\sin C}\{\sin A\sin(C - B)\} = c^2 - b^2
\]
(for this is $\sin A\sin(C - B) = \sin^2 C - \sin^2 B$), we have
\[
\phi x = \frac{c^2 - b^2 - cx}{\sqrt{c(c - x) - b^2}},
\]
as it should be.

If in the formula for $\phi^n x$ we write $x = 0$, we have
\[
\phi^n 0 = \frac{a^2\sin nC}{a\sin(nC - B)},
\]
a formula given in the Senate-House Problems, January 14, 1878: it was thus that I was led to investigate the general expression.

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 688: GEOMETRICAL CONSIDERATIONS ON A SOLAR ECLIPSE
% ============================================================
\section[Geometrical Considerations on a Solar Eclipse]{688. Geometrical Considerations on a Solar Eclipse.\footnote{From the \textit{Quarterly Journal of Pure and Applied Mathematics}, vol.\ XV. (1878), pp.\ 145--149.}}

[310]

I consider, from a geometrical point of view, the phenomena of a solar eclipse over the earth generally; attending at present only to the penumbral cone, the vertex of which I denote by $V$. It is convenient to regard the earth as fixed, and the sun and moon as moving each of them with its proper motion, and also with the diurnal motion. The penumbral cone meets the earth's surface in a curve which may be called the penumbral curve; viz.\ when the cone is not completely traversed by the earth's surface, (that is, when only some of the generating lines of the cone meet the earth's surface), this is a horse-shoe shaped oval; the penumbra is a region bounded by the two branches of the oval, or say these are separated, the one being the rising, the other the setting branch of the penumbral curve.

It is to be noticed that the whole of the oval may be visible, or the whole invisible, or only a portion may be visible; and moreover the character of the oval may change from visible to invisible, or \textit{vice vers\^a}, according as it does or does not intersect the horizon of any given place on the earth's surface.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{1.}\quad
I attend in the first instance to the penumbral curve, and its relation to the horizon at any given place on the earth's surface. I say that the penumbral curve and the horizon have a common tangent $TT'$; viz.\ this is the line of intersection of the horizon-plane by a plane through the axis of the cone (that is, through $V$ and the centre of the moon) parallel to the horizon-plane; or, what is the same thing, it is the line of intersection of the horizon-plane by a plane through the centre of the earth parallel to the plane through the axis of the cone and the horizon-plane.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{2.}\quad
Consider now any point $P$ on the horizon; through $P$ draw a line $PT$ in the direction $T$ of the common tangent; and let $\theta$ denote the angle which the generating line $VP$ of the cone makes with $PT$; then the condition in order that the generating line may meet the earth's surface is that $\cos\theta$ may be between the limits $\pm\cos\theta_0$, where $\theta_0$ is a given angle; and if $\cos\theta$ is within these limits, then the generating line does meet the earth's surface, or does not meet it, according as $P$ lies on the one side or the other of the tangent line $TT'$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{3.}\quad
Hence, taking $P$ a point on the horizon in the direction $T$ of the common tangent, and drawing lines $PQ$ in directions making with $PT$ angles between the limits $\pm\theta_0$, the generating lines $VPQ$ will meet the earth's surface; and we thus see that the points $Q$ form a region bounded by the conic which is the intersection of the earth's surface by the plane through $V$ touching the horizon-plane along the line $TT'$. But if the angle $PTQ$ is outside the limits, then the generating line does not meet the earth's surface, and the corresponding point $Q$ is on the sky.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.}\quad
The foregoing completely determines the visibility or invisibility of any given point of the penumbral curve, and it is easy to see how the character of the penumbral curve as visible or invisible changes as the position of the place on the earth's surface varies. For as the place moves on the earth's surface, the tangent line $TT'$ moves in the plane of the penumbral curve, and the character of any given point changes as the tangent line passes over it; viz.\ the point changes from visible to invisible or \textit{vice vers\^a}, as the tangent line passes over it in such wise that the angle $PTQ$ passes from within to without the limits, or \textit{vice vers\^a}.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{5.}\quad
The vertex $V$ (considered as a point in the heavens) is rising, for a point on the one side of the horizon-envelope, and setting, for a point on the other of them; and the two points $K_1$, $K_2$ are the northern and southern limits respectively. There is, as before, no passage through a point $W$ or $W'$, the curve in question thus retains throughout the same character; and by consideration of the two terminal points it at once appears that the two portions $K_1W$, $K_2W'$ (or $K_1W'$, $K_2W$ as the case may be) are the northern and southern limits respectively.

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 689: ON THE GEOMETRICAL REPRESENTATION OF IMAGINARY VARIABLES
% ============================================================
\section[On the Geometrical Representation of Imaginary Variables by a Real Correspondence of Two Planes]{689. On the Geometrical Representation of Imaginary Variables by a Real Correspondence of Two Planes.\footnote{From the \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, vol.\ IX. (1878), pp.\ 31--39. Read December 13, 1877.}}

[316]

In my recently published paper, ``Geometrical Illustration of a Theorem relating to an Irrational Function of an Imaginary Variable,'' \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, t.\ VIII. (1877), pp.\ 212--214, [627], I remark as follows:---``If we have $v$ a function of $u$ determined by an equation $f(u, v) = 0$, then to any given imaginary value $x + iy$ of $u$ there belong two or more values, in general imaginary, and for the complete understanding of the relation between the two imaginary variables we require to know the series of values $x' + iy'$ which correspond to a given series of values $x + iy$ of $u$; or, what is the same thing, we require to have a geometrical representation of the one series of points by means of the other series of points.'' I propose in the present paper to develop this idea.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{1.}\quad
I consider first the general case of a $(m, n)$ correspondence between two planes; viz.\ to any given point $P$ in the first plane there correspond $n$ points $P'$ in the second plane; and to any given point $P'$ in the second plane there correspond $m$ points $P$ in the first plane. The correspondence is determined by an equation
\[
\Phi(x, y; x', y') = 0,
\]
where $\Phi$ is of the degree $m$ in $(x, y)$ and of the degree $n$ in $(x', y')$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{2.}\quad
To a given point $P$ in the first plane there correspond $n$ points $P'$ in the second plane; and these may be distinct or some of them may be united. In the general case they are distinct; but if the point $P$ is on a certain curve, the branch-curve, then two or more of the corresponding points $P'$ unite together. The branch-curve is a curve on the first plane; and in like manner there is a branch-curve on the second plane.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{3.}\quad
If the point $P$ describes on the first plane a closed curve, the $n$ corresponding points $P'$ describe on the second plane $n$ closed curves; but these may be such that the series of values is not restored, viz.\ the point $P$ may return to its original position, but the $n$ points $P'$ may have been permuted among themselves. To avoid this, we introduce the notion of barriers; viz.\ we draw on the first plane a system of barriers, such that the point $P$ cannot pass from one side to the other of any barrier; and in like manner we draw on the second plane a system of barriers. The barriers are so drawn that when $P$ returns to its original position, each of the $n$ points $P'$ also returns to its original position.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.}\quad
The branch-curve on the first plane consists of a system of points $V$ and of barriers; the point $P$ cannot pass through a point $V$, and it cannot cross a barrier. In like manner the branch-curve on the second plane consists of a system of points $V'$ and of barriers. And we have on the first plane a system of counter-barriers corresponding to the barriers on the second plane; and on the second plane a system of counter-barriers corresponding to the barriers on the first plane.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{5.}\quad
If there were only a single barrier $OV_{12}$, then we might have through $P$ a closed curve cutting $OV_{12}$ once only, and including within it the two portions of a bifid curve.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{6.}\quad
[Further development of the theory of branch points, barriers, and the correspondence between the two planes.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{7.}\quad
The question may be considered in itself, without any reference to such origin.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{8.}\quad
[Discussion of the analytical representation.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{9.}\quad
Everything is of course symmetrical as regards the two planes; we have therefore, in the second plane, a system of points $V'$ and of barriers, and in the first plane a system of points $(W)$ and of counter-barriers. To a given point $P'$ in the second plane there correspond $m$ points $P$ in the first plane; and we can (the determination depending on the system of barriers in the second plane) assign to the $m$ points suffixes, thereby distinguishing them as the corresponding points $P_1$, $P_2$, \dots, $P_m$. And we may imagine the second plane as consisting of $m$ superimposed planes or sheets, say the sheets $1$, $2$, $3$, \dots, $m$; the general theorem then is that to a point $P$ or $P'$ in either plane, considered as a point $P_a$ or $P'_a$ in the sheet $a$ or $a'$, there corresponds in the other plane one and only one point $P'_{a'}$ or $P_{a'}$; and that the first-mentioned point in either plane moving continuously in any manner (subject to the proper change of sheet), the corresponding point in the other plane will also move continuously, and will return to its original position and sheet, upon the first-mentioned point returning to its original position and sheet.

This is, in fact, Riemann's theory, only instead of the points $P'$ we must speak of the values $x' + iy'$ of the irrational function of $x + iy$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{10.}\quad
In all that precedes it has been assumed that, to a branch-point $V$, there correspond two united points represented by $(W)$ and $n - 2$ distinct points $W'$; the cases of a point $(W)$ composed of three or more united points, or of the points $W'$ uniting themselves in sets in any other manner, would give rise to further specialities.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{11.}\quad
[Further discussion of the correspondence theory and its relation to Riemann surfaces.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{12.}\quad
[Consideration of the case where the branch curve has multiple points.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{13.}\quad
[Concluding remarks on the geometrical theory.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{14.}\quad
[The paper concludes with remarks on the application to the theory of functions.]

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 690: ON THE THEORY OF GROUPS
% ============================================================
\section[On the Theory of Groups]{690. On the Theory of Groups.\footnote{From the \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, t.\ IX. (1878), pp.\ 126--133. Read May 9, 1878.}}

[325]

I recapitulate the general theory so far as is necessary in order to render intelligible the quasi-geometrical representation of it which will be given.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{1.}\quad
Let $\alpha$, $\beta$, \dots\ be functional symbols each operating upon one and the same number of letters, and producing as its result the same number of functions of these letters. For instance,
\[
\alpha(x, y, z) = (X, Y, Z),
\]
where the capitals denote each of them a given function of $(x, y, z)$.

Such symbols are susceptible of repetition and combination; thus
\[
\alpha^2(x, y, z) = \alpha(X, Y, Z),\quad \text{or}\quad
\beta\alpha(x, y, z) = \beta(X, Y, Z),
\]
in each case equal to three given functions of $(x, y, z)$; and similarly for $\alpha^3$, $\alpha^2\beta$, \dots, $\alpha\beta\alpha$, \&c.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{2.}\quad
The functional symbols may be of any form whatever, but I consider in particular those which arise from the substitutions of a group of letters; and using in the first instance a set of non-specialized functional symbols, I establish the notion of a group.

A set of symbols
\[
1,\ \alpha,\ \beta,\ \dots
\]
(such that the number of symbols is finite), forming a group, if they are such that the product of any two of them (in either order) is a symbol of the set, and the inverse of each symbol is also a symbol of the set.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{3.}\quad
The number of symbols is called the \textit{order} of the group.

A group may be represented by means of the substitutions of a set of letters; and conversely, any group of substitutions gives rise to a group in the foregoing sense.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.}\quad
To explain the hemihedron, certain configurations by the diagram, as presently appearing, may (instead of any other equivalent representation) be employed.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{5.}\quad
[The paper discusses colorings of polygons associated with the group.]

The polygons may have different colours; the polygons being assigned colors according to group-theoretic principles. The colors of the polygons correspond to the different operations of the group, and the adjacency relations of the colored polygons reflect the group structure.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{6.}\quad
[Further development of the theory of group representations by colorings.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{7.}\quad
[Applications to particular groups and their geometric representations.]

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 691: NOTE ON MR MONRO'S PAPER
% ============================================================
\section[Note on Mr Monro's Paper ``On Flexure of Spaces'']{691. Note on Mr Monro's Paper ``On Flexure of Spaces''.\footnote{From the \textit{Proceedings of the London Mathematical Society}, vol.\ IX. (1878), pp.\ 171, 172. Read June 13, 1878.}}

[331]

Consider an element of surface, surrounding a point $P$; the flexure of the element may be interfered with by the continuity round $P$, and on this account it is proper to regard the element as cut or slit along a radius drawn from $P$ to the periphery of the element. This being understood, we have the well-known theorem that, considering in the neighbourhood of the origin elements of the surfaces
\[
z = \tfrac{1}{2}(ax^2 + 2hxy + by^2),\qquad
z' = \tfrac{1}{2}(a'x'^2 + 2h'x'y' + b'y'^2),
\]
these will be applicable to be the one on the other, provided only $ab - h^2 = a'b' - h'^2$.

But Mr Monro's paper considers the more general problem of the flexure of spaces, and establishes certain differential equations which must be satisfied. The equation will be satisfied identically as regards $dx$, $dy$ if the appropriate conditions on the coefficients are met.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{[The note continues with remarks on the differential equations of flexure.]}

The conditions for the flexure may be expressed by saying that the element at any point $P$ must be applicable upon the element at the corresponding point $P'$, or that the two elements must have the same measure of curvature at corresponding points.

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 692: ADDITION TO THE MEMOIR ON THE TRANSFORMATION OF ELLIPTIC FUNCTIONS
% ============================================================
\section[Addition to the Memoir on the Transformation of Elliptic Functions]{692. Addition to the Memoir on the Transformation of Elliptic Functions.\footnote{From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London}, vol.\ CLXIX., Part II. (1878), pp.\ 419--424. Received February 6,---Read March 7, 1878.}}

[333]

I have recently succeeded in completing a theory considered in my ``Memoir on the Transformation of Elliptic Functions,'' \textit{Phil.\ Trans.}, vol.\ CLXIV. (1874), pp.\ 397--456, [578],---that of the septic transformation, $n = 7$.

We have here
\[
\frac{1 + y}{1 - y} = \frac{1 - z}{1 + z}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha + \beta x + \gamma x^2 - \delta x^3}{\alpha + \beta x + \gamma x^2 + \delta x^3}},
\]
a solution of
\[
\frac{Mdy}{\sqrt{1 - y^2\cdot 1 - \lambda^2y^2}} = \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2\cdot 1 - k^2x^2}},
\]
where
\[
M = 1 + 2\beta\delta,
\]
and the ratios $\alpha : \beta : \gamma : \delta$, and the $\omega v$-modular equation are determined by the equations
\begin{align*}
\alpha(\beta\gamma + \alpha\delta) &= u^2v^2(\gamma\delta + 2\beta\gamma + 2\alpha\delta),\\
\gamma^2 + 2\beta\gamma\delta + 2\alpha\delta^2 &= u^2v^2(\beta\gamma\delta + 2\beta\gamma + 2\alpha\delta + \beta^2).
\end{align*}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{1.}\quad
The septic transformation presents itself naturally as a development of the theory of modular equations. The equation
\[
T\eta v = 1 + 2/\delta,
\]
that is, certain modular equations are presented which connect the moduli $k$ and $\lambda$.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{2.}\quad
[The paper develops the theory of the septic transformation.]

The modular equations present remarkable symmetries. Writing down the equations for the transformation, we obtain relations between the coefficients which may be expressed as follows:

\begin{align*}
&(ca' - c'a) = uv + uv(u^6 - 10u^4v^2 + 11u^2v^4 - 6v^6 - 8u^6 + 10u^4v^2 - 4v^6)\\
&\quad + u^2v^2(-4u^6 + 10u^4v^2 - 8u^2v^4 - 6u^6 + 11u^4v^2 - 10u^2v^4 + 6v^6) + u^3v^3.
\end{align*}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{3.}\quad
The third and fourth equations should then be verified identically. Writing down the equations:

\begin{align*}
&-64 + 224 - 352 + 224 + 160 - 392 + 160 + 224 - 352 + 224 - 64\\
&-1 \qquad 0 + 16 \qquad 0 - 48 \qquad 0 - 48 \qquad 0 + 16 \qquad 0 - 1
\end{align*}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{4.}\quad
[Further identities are developed:]

viz.\ this is
\[
+ (-4u^6 + 6u^4v^2 - 4u^2v^4)\, 2(u^6 + v^6) + 16u^8 - 48u^6v^2 + 68u^4v^4 - 48u^2v^6 + 16v^8.
\]

The coefficients in the expansion satisfy the symmetry conditions required by the theory of transformation.

\newpage
% ============================================================
% PAPER 693: A TENTH MEMOIR ON QUANTICS
% ============================================================
\section[A Tenth Memoir on Quantics]{693. A Tenth Memoir on Quantics.\footnote{From the \textit{Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society of London}, vol.\ CLXIX., Part II. (1878), pp.\ 603--661. Received June 12,---Read June 20, 1878.}}

[339]

The Memoir, which relates to the binary quintic $(\xi x, y)^5$, has been in hand for a considerable time: the chief subject-matter was intended to be the theory of a canonical form which was discovered by myself and is briefly noticed in Salmon's \textit{Higher Algebra}, 3rd Ed.\ (1876), pp.\ 217, 218; writing $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$, $e$, $f$, $g$, \dots, $u$, $v$, $w$ to denote the 23 covariants of the quintic, then $a$, $b$, $c$, $d$, $f$ are connected by the relation
\[
f^2 = -a^3 + a^2bc - 4c^3;
\]
and the form contains these covariants thus connected together, and also $e$; it, in fact, is
\[
(a, 0, c, f)^3(a^2e - 3c^2, a^4e - 2cd\,|\,x, y)^2.
\]
But the whole plan of the Memoir was altered by the discoveries of Prof.\ Sylvester relating to the generating functions of the quintic, and the Memoir thus came to contain a much more complete theory of the quintic than was originally contemplated.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Art.\ Nos.\ 366 to 374. Table No.\ 96.}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{366.}\quad
I have, in my Ninth Memoir (1871) [462], given what may be called the theory of the generating functions of the quintic. Sylvester obtained an expression for the N.G.F.\ of the quintic which is discussed here.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{367.}\quad
The memoir continues with the discussion of deg-orders and concomitants of the quintic. Sylvester obtained an expression for the N.G.F.\ (numerical generating function) of the quintic, and from this deduced the number and nature of the concomitants.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{368.}\quad
The generating function for the quintic presents remarkable properties. The B.G.F.\ (binomial generating function) of the quintic gives rise to a complete enumeration of the concomitants. The deg-orders of the irreducible concomitants are exhibited in the following table:

\begin{center}
\textbf{B.O.F.\ of quintic}
\medskip
\begin{tabular}{|c|l|}
\hline
Deg-orders & \multicolumn{1}{c|}{Concomitants} \\
\hline
0.0--10.10 & Fundamental form $(\xi x, y)^5$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{369.}\quad
[The enumeration of covariants continues with detailed tables.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{370.}\quad
The number of irreducible covariants and their deg-orders are systematically determined. The generating function approach yields a complete classification.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{371.}\quad
The tables of deg-orders are verified by independent methods.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{372.}\quad
[The memoir presents extensive tables of concomitants.]

Observe as regards the foregoing diagram, that $dj^2$ [nor $j^2$] segregate in certain cases; the syzygies connecting the different covariants are exhibited in detail.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{373.}\quad
\textbf{[Syzygies]}

The 17 syzygies above referred to are

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\hline
Deg-ord. & Order \\
\hline
6.10 & 6 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

[Further syzygies of higher degree and order are enumerated.]

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{374.}\quad
Table No.\ 96 (continued). The complete table of concomitants of the quintic includes the generating functions, the deg-orders, and the syzygies which connect them. The memoir concludes with a verification that the number of irreducible concomitants is precisely equal to the number indicated by the generating function.
